GN Store Nord · GN-DK · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: GN Store Nord

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 43,854 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

External headwinds/tailwinds

F4064th of 79

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is a cyclical trough with a turn already visible in North America and APAC, sitting on a structurally slower and more contested base. The second derivative is positive: growth improved from -7% to +1% in Enterprise and from -4% to +2% for RemainCo across 2026, and the tariff headwinds have reversed. The level is still poor: Europe, which is about half of revenue, has not turned, Europe's consumers and interest rates are hostile, and the structural forces (longer replacement cycles, AI software budgets, bundling, software noise suppression, low-cost certified entrants) push slowly in the wrong direction. Visibility is under two years.

For the Consumer, three things follow:

  1. Over the next 6-9 months, external factors flatter reported growth (easy comparisons, destocking ending).
  2. 2028 growth must rest on genuine end demand, which is the weakest-evidenced part of the external picture.
  3. The range of outcomes is wide and skewed slightly to the downside, because Europe and the consumer can still weaken.
1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0240C-0545C-0730C-0950C-3540C-3955
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

D4047th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising moderately overall. It rises most where GN is weakest (European entry-level headsets, bundled corporate tenders and lower-tier gaming mice and keyboards) and is stable or easing at the premium end, where Jabra is taking share.

  • Rising pressure.
  • Yealink is expanding into Europe at low B2B prices and is "rapidly closing the hardware feature gap" (Jabra innovation expert, 24 August 2026).
  • Chinese and unbranded players hold about 12% of enterprise headset revenue.
  • Logitech has raised European promotional spending and discounts video hardware "down to cost" to win managed-services revenue (Jabra video expert, 28 August 2026).
  • Logitech, HP, Dell and Lenovo increasingly sell bundled workspace packages to corporate accounts (DNB Carnegie, 9 November 2026).
  • In gaming, low-cost Asian brands undercut mid-to-low-tier mice and keyboards by 30-50%, and Logitech and Corsair typically cut prices 10-15% in slowdowns.
  • Easing pressure.
  • HP Poly's discounting of up to 80% after the acquisition diluted its brand, and its messy integration let Jabra take share (CDW and former Poly and Logitech experts).
  • The 2025 tariff price increases went through industry-wide (Logitech up to 25% in the US, HP Poly about 5% on average selling prices), which is rational oligopoly behaviour.
  • Logitech's semiconductor supply problem (up to USD 200m of lost revenue in its Q3 FY27) temporarily eases gaming competition.
  • The last 6 months against the prior 12-18: the bundling and Chinese-entrant themes have strengthened in broker and expert commentary through 2026, and European entry-level share loss is now named as a cause of GN's double-digit European decline.

For the model: positive price and mix at the premium end, and a share drag at European entry level. Do not model a price war in the core; do model a structurally lower European entry-level share.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C4529th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: input costs are mixed. Tariff relief offsets component inflation, so the net effect over the next 6-12 months is a slight headwind. The bigger issue is concentration and availability of supply (semiconductors, Taiwan) rather than price.

  • Easing costs.
  • Tariff costs are falling: relocation is complete, the 2025 one-offs are gone and refunds have been received.
  • Consolidating mechanical sub-suppliers from 37 to 15 since 2022 cut legacy costs by 5-10%.
  • Consensus forecasts mild US dollar weakening (EUR/USD 1.15-1.16). The dollar is a net cost currency at EBITA level (35% of cost of goods and 45% of operating costs, against 39% of group revenue; FY2023 data), so a weaker dollar is a small tailwind once the 12-month rolling hedges roll off. The files disagree on the sign of the profit sensitivity, so treat it as small.
  • Rising costs.
  • Memory and semiconductor inflation: Bernstein, Deutsche Bank and Morgan Stanley flag memory shortages lifting device prices 15-20% and squeezing B2B hardware margins in 2026. Logitech expects margin pressure from memory costs.
  • The Chinese yuan is expected to strengthen (USD/CNY 6.70-6.76). Yuan costs were 20% of cost of goods in 2023 and are lower after relocation.
  • Southeast Asian hubs bring new local-currency costs with no matching revenue.
  • Availability and concentration.
  • Logitech's disruption at a microcontroller and wireless-chip supplier (July 2026) shows the availability risk in this supply chain.
  • Short-range wireless chips (Nordic, Airoha) depend on TSMC.
  • GN uses about 150 component suppliers and outsources 7 of its 9 manufacturing sites.
  • The current outcome is favourable. Underlying RemainCo gross margin was about 51.7-51.9% in Q2-Q3 2026, against 49.6% for FY2025. Pricing and tariff mitigation currently beat cost inflation.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3069th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro forces are a material headwind. They bite hardest at company level, where about 50% European revenue, a high fixed-cost base and destocking driven by interest rates compound one another, and in valuation at sector level.

  • The macro inputs.
  • The European Central Bank raised rates twice in 2026 (to 2.50% in September), and Deutsche Bank expects a 2.75% deposit rate in December. Euro-area inflation was 3.8% in September 2026.
  • Consumer confidence is weak and still edging lower (German GfK -30.8; euro-area flash -17.9).
  • Oil is near $100 and carries Iran supply risk. The US 10-year yield is near 5%.
  • US corporate spending is stable. The IMF raised its world growth forecast.
  • Company level.
  • European corporate IT budgets are the swing factor for Enterprise.
  • High rates raise distributors' carrying costs, so they hold less stock.
  • Gaming depends on household discretionary spending.
  • Operating leverage of about 40-60% drop-through magnifies each point of revenue (C-75).
  • Currency: the dollar weakening mildly is a translation headwind on about 30% North American revenue and a small transaction tailwind. The krone's peg neutralises the euro.
  • Interest rates turn from a cost (group net interest DKK 645m in 2025) into a small income once GN holds net cash, then back towards a cost as it re-levers to 1.0-1.5x.
  • Sector and industry level. The Consumer products sector median fell 3.4% over 12 months. Viewforth's environment reading: Consumer discretionary Double Underweight, Consumer products Underweight, Fragility Double Underweight, regime risk-off. The binding mechanism is the discount rate. That weighs on GN's multiple whatever its operations do.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–50

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four other external dynamics matter. Two lean negative and two positive. None is in the operating model directly, but all bear on value per share.

  1. Amplifon's vertical integration of GN Hearing (negative or uncertain). Independent audiologists resent the deal and may move orders away from ReSound, and Amplifon's multiple has de-rated. GN's 56 million Amplifon shares (worth DKK 4.4bn at announcement) and their sale after lock-up fund part of the distributions. So a fall in Amplifon's share price directly cuts GN's capital-return capacity. This is the cross-company loop to watch.
  2. Distributor power and concentration (negative). A single distributor generated DKK 2,001m of 2025 sales, mostly in Enterprise. That is about 21% of RemainCo revenue on my arithmetic. Distributors run thin margins, demand marketing funds and move inventory with interest rates, which amplifies GN's quarterly volatility.
  3. William Demant Invest's stake of about 12.3% (two-sided). Its strategic logic, a possible hearing combination, disappeared with the sale. Management has had to answer whether the Demant Foundation is restricted to healthcare holdings. A non-strategic holder is a potential seller. The DKK 4bn buyback could absorb it, as a block or in the market. This is conjecture.
  4. Logitech's supply disruption (positive, temporary). It opens holiday-season shelf space in gaming mice and keyboards. One report estimates DKK 100-150m of possible revenue if the disruption persists through Q4. That is speculative.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F4060th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: new-entrant threats are real but uneven. Standard low-cost entrants are the most immediate threat (medium-high at entry and mid-tier). Adjacent PC makers using bundling are a medium threat. Orthogonal software and consumer-electronics entrants are a medium, slower threat. Platform owners making their own hardware are a low threat.

Type of entrant Who Mechanism Threat
Standard (low-cost) Yealink, Shenzhen vendors, Xiaomi in gaming Expanding in Europe, closing the feature gap; certification is a barrier but entrants such as Yealink already hold it Medium-high
Adjacent Dell, Lenovo, HP Bundling PCs, monitors and peripherals Medium
Orthogonal Krisp, operating-system AI; Apple and Samsung earbuds Software substitution; low-end use for work Medium, slower
Platform owners Microsoft, Zoom Designing room computing boxes, but staying hardware-agnostic Low

The business-foundations digest warns that the B2B peripheral industry "could undergo structural commoditisation if low-cost Chinese entrants successfully certify high-quality, software-agnostic hardware at a fraction of western price points".

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C5526th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are on balance a support. Outsourced contract manufacturers are flexible, sub-suppliers are consolidated at lower cost, and payment terms are long. The risks are concentrated in semiconductors (memory and wireless chips, Taiwan) and in the loss of the Hearing division's purchasing scale.

  • Support.
  • Seven of nine manufacturing sites are fully outsourced to tier-1 contract manufacturers, which can move lines within 8-12 weeks.
  • Sub-suppliers were consolidated from 37 to 15, raising average volume per supplier from 23 million to 56 million units and cutting legacy costs by 5-10%.
  • A 2023 contract with a major manufacturing and logistics partner extended payment terms.
  • Products flow through 11 distribution hubs run by one fourth-party logistics provider.
  • Every major product can be supplied from at least two countries.
  • Risk.
  • More than 150 component suppliers, with high-end audio dependent on Asian semiconductors made in Taiwan.
  • The memory shortage.
  • Onerous purchase commitments, which forced inventory provisions in Q1 2026.
  • Smaller purchasing volume after Hearing leaves. Management stresses that more than 20 million units a year keeps GN a preferred customer.
  • New costs in Vietnamese dong without matching revenue.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0136C-0342C-0448C-0846C-3242C-3754C-4348
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3668th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end demand is at a cyclical trough with an early turn that is split by region, sitting on a structurally slower replacement-driven base. Visibility is well under two years, so this does not qualify as a cyclical swing with two-plus years of visibility.

  • Enterprise (about 72% of RemainCo) has turned, but only in North America and APAC.
  • Organic growth was negative for 14 consecutive quarters from 2023 to Q2 2026 (2025: -9%, -7%, -4%, -3%; 2026: -5%, -7%), then +1% in Q3 2026.
  • North America and APAC grew at double digits in Q1 2026, and North American business-channel sell-out has been positive for four consecutive quarters.
  • Europe, Middle East and Africa fell at double digits in H1 2026 on distributor destocking and entry-level competition, and was described as "challenging but improved" in Q3.
  • The turn is therefore real in the Americas and APAC and still unproven in Europe, which is about half of RemainCo revenue.
  • The structural base is slower than the 2010s.
  • Knowledge-worker headset replacement cycles have stretched to 4-5 years from 2-3; contact-centre cycles stay at 18-20 months.
  • Chief information officers are diverting budgets to AI software; survey data cap enterprise video-conferencing spend growth at 2.7%.
  • DNB models long-term enterprise market growth of 3-5%.
  • Against this, headsets bought in the 2020-21 home-office boom are reaching end-of-life, so a replacement wave is mechanically due over 2025-27. Premium Evolve3 sell-out growing at double digits is the first evidence that it is starting.
  • Price, refill or use. Most of the ~10% US price increase of mid-2025 has been lapped by Q3 2026, so Q3's +1% is closer to volume and mix than H1's figures were. The Consumer should check the exact date the increase took effect.
  • Sell-out ran ahead of sell-in through H1 2026, so the end of destocking lifts sell-in up to sell-out without a restocking surplus.
  • The sector digest's rule applies: higher interest rates keep distributor stocks permanently lower, so the recovery is smaller and slower, with no restocking boom.
  • Gaming (about 28%) is premium-resilient but tied to the consumer. Organic growth was +11%, 0%, +3%, -12% in 2025 and -1%, +5%, +5% in 2026. Consumer confidence is weak (German GfK -30.8 in November 2026). The upside triggers are product cycles (Arctis Nova Pro Omni) and big game releases (the files describe Grand Theft Auto 6 as upcoming).
  • FalCom defence has structural demand but lumpy timing: 2026 expectations were cut to DKK 150m from DKK 200m as projects slipped into 2027.

For the model: base RemainCo organic growth of low-to-mid single digits for 2027, with North America and APAC leading and Europe near flat. Put a wide range of about ±5 points around it. Q4 2026 and H1 2027 growth is flattered by easy comparisons (Q4 2025: Enterprise -3%, Gaming -12%).

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F4260th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the main disruption force, software-based AI noise suppression in operating systems and unified-communications platforms, is strengthening as a capability but has not yet shown up in demand. Its direction is negative for GN over 3-5 years and roughly neutral over the next 12 months.

  • The threat. Microsoft Teams, Zoom, Apple macOS and Krisp-style software now suppress noise. If software reaches parity with hardware signal processing, the premium a buyer pays for a Jabra headset erodes. The reports put this on a 24-36 month horizon. A related substitute is the growing use of consumer earbuds for work.
  • GN's counter, and the evidence against near-term disruption.
  • Evolve3 embeds deep-neural-network silicon (ClearVoice, which captures 99% of spoken words) to keep a hardware edge.
  • Premium Bluetooth headsets (about 15-20% of Enterprise) grew sell-out at double digits in 2026, and Evolve3 75/85 premium revenue grew over 50%.
  • The premium segment is exactly where software substitution would bite first, so this is evidence the threat is not yet in the numbers.
  • Positive technology shifts. Bluetooth LE Audio and Auracast are an industry refresh trigger that favours early adopters (GN is one). An AI-led workplace in which voice becomes the input to AI agents could raise the value of high-quality microphones. That second point is conjecture: no evidence of demand yet.
  • Platform owners as hardware rivals. Microsoft and Zoom have stayed hardware-agnostic (Microsoft "remains committed to buying third-party hardware").

The monitorable: the memo's kill criterion, evidence that native operating-system noise cancellation is displacing premium hardware in enterprise tenders.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D4845th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the political and regulatory picture has moved from a clear headwind in 2025 to roughly neutral in 2026, with a one-off windfall. The skew of what comes next is to the downside, because trade policy is volatile and the relocated factories still face tariffs of 19-20%.

  • Tariffs, the dominant factor.
  • US tariffs of about 20% on Enterprise and Gaming shipments cost about 100bp of net operating margin in 2025. About half of that was one-off relocation expense that does not recur in 2026.
  • GN moved 31 production lines out of China in 2025 (19 Enterprise, 12 Gaming), so about 90% of US-bound volume now ships from Vietnam, Malaysia, Thailand, Taiwan, Germany and the US. Every major product can be made in at least two countries, and contract manufacturers can move a line in 8-12 weeks.
  • Residual US tariffs of 20% on Vietnam and 19% on Malaysia leave about a 30bp margin headwind for 2026 (Bernstein).
  • The DKK 153m US tariff refund booked in Q3 2026 is a one-off.
  • Defence spending. Rising European and NATO budgets support FalCom (2025 breakthrough of about DKK 200m; 2026 expectation DKK 150m after slippage).
  • EU product regulation. The battery passport (February 2027), ecodesign rules, right-to-repair and sustainability reporting add modest, ongoing design and compliance cost.
  • Hearing-only risks. Section 232 on medical imports and Medicaid and reimbursement changes no longer affect RemainCo directly. They matter only through the value of the Amplifon stake.

For the model: treat 2026's refund as non-recurring. Keep the residual about 30bp of tariff drag in 2026 and 2027. Hold a downside scenario of 100-200bp if US tariffs on Southeast Asian hubs rise.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F4665th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: no industry or sector driver is clearly accelerating GN's demand today. The strongest near-term force is mechanical: distributors finishing their destocking (nearly done in North America, not in Europe). It returns sell-in to sell-out but does not lift demand above it.

Candidate driver Status Effect on GN
Replacement of the 2020-21 hybrid-work installed base Real and starting (premium double-digit sell-out) Diluted by 4-5 year cycles
PC refresh (Windows 11) HP commercial PCs +7% in late 2025, but memory inflation now cutting PC units (HP low-double-digit unit declines) Weak support for headset attach rates in 2026
Defence (FalCom) Accelerating structurally, lumpy Small: about 1.5-2% of revenue
AI-led workplace and voice Talk, no demand evidence yet None measurable
Gaming releases and Logitech's supply gap Episodic Holiday-season share gains
Bluetooth LE Audio / Auracast Multi-year standard shift Refresh catalyst; timing unclear
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F4268th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the thesis in North American and APAC premium enterprise and in premium gaming headsets. It does not yet support it in Europe or in mass-market gaming. The support is fragile because it depends on a replacement cycle that customers can defer.

  • Supportive.
  • North American B2B sell-out has been positive for four consecutive quarters.
  • Premium Bluetooth sell-out has grown at double digits since Evolve3's launch, and mid-tier sell-in grew at double digits in Q3.
  • Corporate buyers showed "strong enthusiasm" for Evolve3.
  • The customer base is diversified: over 20,000 resellers, more than 80% of Fortune 100 companies, and no single end customer of material size.
  • Fragile.
  • Corporate buyers defer upgrades: cycles are now 4-5 years, and CIOs prioritise AI software and meeting-room video over personal headsets.
  • Office hot-desking reduces individual peripheral purchases.
  • Consumers are cautious and trade down; mouse demand is weak.
  • What the customer wants. Fewer IT problems: 77% of US office workers face weekly technology issues (Jabra's own report, August 2026, so a marketing source). That supports premium, managed, certified devices.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C-5437th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and politicians currently help slightly. US tariff policy is now giving refunds, GN's relocation has been absorbed, and defence procurement is growing. Risks are rising mildly in EU product-compliance cost and in trade-policy volatility. The position is fragile because tariff policy can move against Southeast Asian hubs at short notice.

  • Helping.
  • The US tariff refunds (DKK 153m in Q3 2026).
  • Rising NATO and European defence budgets (FalCom).
  • No antitrust constraint on RemainCo.
  • The Hearing sale has cleared all approvals.
  • Hurting.
  • Residual US tariffs of 19-20% on Vietnam and Malaysia.
  • Possible secondary US tariffs on alternative hubs; the risks report sets a Vietnam tariff of 20% as a trigger for re-running the pre-mortem, a level now reached.
  • The EU battery passport (February 2027), ecodesign rules and right-to-repair raise design cost for SteelSeries and Jabra.
  • Fragility. Moderate-to-high: one executive order can reverse 2026's relief.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4860th of 75

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is about DKK 70-80bn, roughly 8 times RemainCo's revenue (my sum). In enterprise headsets GN already holds about half its market, so its growth there is close to market growth of 3-5%. Faster growth has to come from video, FalCom and gaming, where GN is a challenger. That supports mid-term organic growth of about 3-6%, in line with DNB's expectation for the CMD target. It does not support a return to the old 5-8% plus.

Market Size GN position
Enterprise headsets ~USD 2.1-2.2bn industry revenue (my inference from GN's ~46% share), ~DKK 14-15bn About half
Meeting-room audio and video DKK 18bn 3-4% share
Tactical communications (FalCom) DKK 5bn A few per cent
Core gaming gear USD 5.5bn, ~DKK 38bn 6-14% share
Total about DKK 75bn RemainCo ~DKK 9.5bn, about 12-13%
  • Market growth. DNB models long-term enterprise market growth at 3-5%. The wider smart audio and video device market is expected to grow 4.3% a year to 2030, and wireless headphones 7.8% a year.
  • Share upside. Video (from 3-4%), FalCom (European rearmament) and gaming headsets and mice.

For the model: a base case of about 4% a year organic over 2027-2029, with a range of about 1-6%. That range is consistent with consensus revenue growth of +4.9%, +4.4% and +4.0%.

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0658C-1040
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5839th of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: RemainCo's own revenue cycle is at or just past its trough and is starting to turn, with moderate confidence. Reported growth will turn sharply (Q4 2026 to H1 2027) for mechanical reasons, but end demand has clearly turned only in North America and APAC.

  • The sequence. RemainCo organic growth was -4%, -4% and +2% in 2026; Enterprise was -5%, -7% and +1%; Gaming -1%, +5% and +5%. Guidance implies about +5-6% in Q4.
  • Why the reported turn will be sharp. Q4 2025 comparisons were weak (Enterprise -3%, Gaming -12%). Evolve3 mid-tier models started shipping in September 2026 (they cover about half of the Enterprise range), and management plans to "accelerate the launch cadence into 2027".
  • Offsetting factors. FalCom deferrals cost about 2.5 points in Q4. GN built inventory in Q3 ahead of expected Q4 sales (a negative DKK 205m working-capital effect). That shows management's confidence, but it becomes a risk if Q4 demand disappoints.
  • What proves the turn is real. Positive Enterprise organic growth in Q4 2026 despite the FalCom drag. Then European stabilisation in H1 2027 against comparisons that are no longer easy (H1 2026 Europe was down double digits, so H1 2027 comparisons are easy too, which means the real test is H2 2027).

For the model: 2027 reported organic growth of 3-6% is reachable mostly on comparisons and destocking ending. 2028 must stand on end demand.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F4062nd of 75

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: external headwinds are moderate-to-strong and broad. Tailwinds are narrower and mostly specific to GN (North American demand, tariff relief, defence). The overall external picture is mildly negative but improving at the margin.

  • Sector themes (Consumer products).
  • "Collectors' systems": not relevant. SteelSeries' GG software (3-4 million active users) is a weak analogue.
  • "Shops and dealers keeping stocks permanently lower": relevant and two-sided. GN gains as each region's channels finish cutting (North America first, Europe later), but there is no restocking boom.
  • "Professional espresso machines": not relevant.
  • The sector rule that "the market pays a high multiple while volume grows, not only price" warns that a recovery led by price would not be rewarded.
  • Industry themes (Consumer discretionary). "Cash returned from a shrinking asset base needs no recovery in demand" applies directly and positively to the buyback. "At trough earnings the P/E misleads" applies to GN's 22x.
  • By level.
Level Headwinds Tailwinds
Value chain European demand, entry-level competition, distributor destocking, component inflation Tariff relief, regional demand recovery
Industry AI software crowding out hardware budgets, bundling, software substitution Installed-base replacement, certification standards
Sector Weak European consumer, risk-off regime, discount rate (none)

Internal change forces/risks

A775th of 79

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internal change is the strongest part of the GN case, and it is overwhelmingly corporate and financial. The Hearing sale turned a leveraged, cyclically exposed conglomerate into a cash-rich pure play. It funds a capital return of at least DKK 4bn (about a quarter of the market value) by end-2027, with a probable second wave from Amplifon proceeds in 2028. It removes about DKK 600m a year of interest cost.

Management has shown decisiveness: it exited consumer earbuds, relocated 31 lines and sold Hearing at a price above the group's own market value. The DKK 200m cost programme is credible given the One GN record. Against this, the same team has repeatedly missed operating guidance, kept the lower-quality businesses, faces risks to talent and organisation, and has not yet said how RemainCo will win.

For the Consumer, the internal forces justify:

  • High confidence in the share-count, interest and tax bridge and in about 2 points of 2027 margin from savings.
  • Moderate confidence in the margin rebuild beyond that.
  • Low confidence in any growth acceleration attributed to strategy rather than to the cycle.
2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement44C-1460C-8628C-5930C-8144D-0380
Group judgement

Risks

C-4431st of 75

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is two-layered. A large, contracted, now regulator-cleared cash floor (about DKK 35-60 a share of non-operating value) sits beneath a cyclical, high-operating-leverage RemainCo whose earnings are at a trough and whose demand turn is one quarter old.

  • Fundamental downside. Concentrated in a European corporate IT freeze, magnified by operating leverage and correlated across both divisions. Also in trade-policy and semiconductor shocks, distributor concentration and Amplifon's share price.
  • Sentiment downside. Elevated. The 21.5% three-month rally on the known buyback catalyst, a sector rated Underweight, overhangs from Demant and Amplifon, and a CMD that will likely lower ambitions all mean the stock could fall even if operations improve.

The fair trough is about DKK 75-90 (22-35% downside). For the Consumer, the downside scenario should combine Europe flat to down, Enterprise growth of -3% to 0%, EBITA at about 7.5-8.5% and Amplifon 25% lower. It should not assume a breach of the cash floor.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C-6032nd of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are clearly better on cost discipline and supply-chain execution. They are not yet better on commercial forecasting and operating delivery. Governance has been tightened. Accounting is temporarily noisier.

  • Better.
  • One GN delivered about DKK 600m of synergies, slightly ahead of plan, before it was abandoned.
  • In 2025 GN moved 31 production lines out of China at speed and, per management, without bottlenecks or quality defects. It now dual-sources every major product.
  • The DKK 200m savings programme is described as completed in its actions, with full run-rate effect in 2027. About 300 back-office roles go and finance is centralised in Warsaw.
  • Free cash flow beat guidance in 2025 (DKK 1,112m against an initial DKK 800m).
  • The share of development cost capitalised was cut to 53% in 2024.
  • Board independence is 100% of shareholder-elected directors, there is a new audit chair, and PwC has issued unqualified opinions.
  • Not better.
  • Guidance is still missed or cut (C-69).
  • Q1 2026 carried inventory provisions from moving US warehouses.
  • Q2 2026 free cash flow was -DKK 616m from bringing supply-chain activities in-house.
  • SteelSeries' IT and ERP integration previously delayed deliveries.
  • The Q3 2026 general and administrative line needed a correction.
  • Earnings quality is temporarily worse because of impairments, refunds, provisions and cost reallocations (C-54).

For the model: treat the DKK 200m saving as high-probability, given the record on cost. Treat revenue guidance as low-reliability, given the record on demand.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F2868th of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negative corporate developments are substantial and mostly present rather than merely plausible. The heaviest are the loss of the defensive earnings stream, the risk to talent and organisation, and the quality of the earnings base.

  1. Higher cyclicality. The remaining earnings stream is wholly cyclical (corporate IT and consumer discretionary), with no defensive cash flow beneath it.
  2. Dis-synergies. DKK 200m of stranded costs must be cut just to stand still against the pre-sale structure. Purchasing scale shrinks, although 20 million units a year remain. Cross-division know-how (FalCom and miniaturisation) is lost.
  3. People. About 300 roles are being cut, finance is being centralised, One GN friction persists, and DNB names key-employee departures the top near-term risk. Board evaluations flagged weak succession. Total employee turnover was 15.2% in 2024.
  4. Separation execution. DKK 750m of cash costs, transitional service agreements with Amplifon for IT and administration, and a history of SteelSeries ERP delays.
  5. Earnings-quality noise. Refunds inside adjusted EBITA, a G&A correction, inventory provisions, capitalised development, impairments (C-54).
  6. Under-investment risk. Consensus capital expenditure including capitalised development falls from 10.7% to 4.6% of revenue by 2029. If that reflects R&D cuts, it endangers the product cadence the moat depends on.
  7. Re-levering a cyclical to fund returns beyond net cash.
  8. Weak alignment. Executives and board own under 0.15% of the shares. 459,225 options were forfeited in 2025 and none were exercised.
  9. Strategic uncertainty. Long-term targets are absent until February 2027, and the market speculates about a break-up.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F3066th of 75

Judgement: the culture is not a holistic, congruent system of the kind seen at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair. Goals, strategy, incentives and execution have repeatedly come apart: strategies abandoned, guidance missed, options forfeited, ownership low. Cost and cash discipline are, however, becoming part of the culture.

  • Total turnover of 15.2% (2024).

  • Congruent elements. Cash discipline (free cash flow above guidance), speed on cost, and a narrower focus.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

A801st of 75

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers are highly concentrated. Two financial drivers, the removal of interest and the fall in the share count, produce about four fifths of the difference between 2028 earnings per share and 2026's. One cost programme and one volume assumption produce the rest. Concentration helps predictability here, because the two largest drivers are contracted, dated and independent of demand. It hurts in two ways. Each large driver has a single point of dependence. And the volume driver is itself concentrated in one region, one product family, one distributor and one quarter of the year.

1. The bridge from 2026 to 2028 (earnings per share after PPA amortisation, before special items: DKK 0.49 to DKK 6.16).

Driver Contribution Share Nature
Net financial items: -540 to -15 about DKK 2.8 49% One step, at closing
Share count: 145.6m to 102.2m on average about DKK 1.8 32% Two programmes: DKK 4bn (to end-2027), DKK 3bn (2028)
Operations: EBITA from 869 to 1,033; lower PPA amortisation about DKK 1.1 19% The DKK 200m cost programme; volume; less the DKK 153m refund of 2026
  • Against the last four to six years, the lines that carry the business barely differ from RemainCo's recent range: revenue growth of 2.5-3.5%, a gross margin of 52%, an EBITA margin of 9-10%. The lines that differ most are below operating profit.
  • The forecast is a different capital structure on much the same business.

2. Why this concentration is mostly good for predictability.

  • Few things must go right, and the largest two need no customer to do anything.
  • The precedents for earnings forecasts resting on buybacks and interest savings split cleanly by how the buyback is funded.
  • Funded from cash already held, it was delivered. Logitech returned 120% of its free cash flow through a downturn. Demant has resumed with DKK 3bn.
  • Funded from cash yet to be earned or from debt, it was cut when operations weakened. Plantronics never began. HP slowed its buybacks once leverage reached 1.8x after buying Poly.
  • GN's programme has all three kinds in sequence, and predictability falls in the same order.
  • 2027: cash in hand. About DKK 4bn, against about DKK 2.5bn of net cash, the first Amplifon tranche and a year's cash flow.
  • 2028: an asset at a market price. The remaining Amplifon shares, about DKK 2.7bn on my value.
  • 2029-2030: debt and cash flow yet to be earned, DKK 3.5bn in the inputs. This is the kind the precedents show being cut.

3. Where concentration is a weakness.

  • Each financial driver has one dependence.
  • Interest depends on closing (expected by end-December 2026).
  • The 2027 share count depends on the annual meeting's authorisation on 10 March 2027.
  • The 2028 share count depends on Amplifon's share price between about September 2027 and March 2028.
  • The drivers are concentrated but not yet agreed. Brokers' 2027 net financial items run from about DKK -3m to DKK -461m, and their implied share counts from about 106m to 129m. The market has not converged on the two drivers that matter most. That is a property of the brokers' models, not of the drivers, and it should narrow once closing has happened (I-02).
  • The volume driver is narrow.
  • Europe is 50% of Enterprise revenue. Enterprise in Europe alone is 36% of group revenue (nine months of 2026).
  • Evolve3 covers about half of Enterprise.
  • One distributor bought DKK 2,001m in 2025, about 21% of revenue.
  • The fourth quarter brings about 31% of the year's revenue and about 35% of its EBITA.
  • Enterprise and Gaming, the only two divisions, both weaken in a European downturn.
  • The financial drivers raise the exposure to the narrow one. By 2028 the same operating profit is divided among 30% fewer shares, so each point of revenue is worth more per share than it is today (P-01). Concentration in mechanical drivers buys predictability for 18 months and sells some of it afterwards.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5049th of 75

Judgement: no chief executive, finance director or chair has changed in the last 18 months. The people are the same but their mandate changed in March 2026, from integrating "One GN" to separating, cutting costs and returning capital. The signal is continuity under a new job description. The CEO's background in B2B technology fits the smaller pure technology company better than it fitted the medtech hybrid.

  • The leadership.
  • Peter Karlstromer has been Group CEO since October 2023 (CEO of GN Audio from January 2023). Before that he was a McKinsey partner for 15 years, Cisco senior vice president for EMEAR for 7 years, and Securitas divisional president for Europe from 2019 to 2022.
  • Søren Jelert has been CFO since June 2023 (previously at Maersk and ALK-Abello).

All three employee representatives are new. Lise Skaarup Mortensen has chaired the audit committee since 2025, and Charlotte Johs brings consumer-technology experience.

  • What the continuity means. The team that missed operating guidance in 2024-26 is the team that must now deliver the 2027 margin rebuild. Continuity lowers execution risk on the carve-out, but there is no fresh-eyes reset of commercial execution. There is no finance-director refresh either; the Wisdom Library treats a new CFO as a leading indicator of a reset in capital discipline, and that signal is absent here.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C6422nd of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has changed more than at any time in a decade. GN has reversed the 2023-24 "One GN" integration and become a pure audio, video and gaming hardware company. The choice is clearly articulated as value crystallisation and is well resourced in cash. The strategy for RemainCo itself is not yet articulated: long-term targets have been suspended since March 2026 and will only be reset at the Capital Markets Day on 24 February 2027.

  • Clear choices.
  • Sell the most defensive, highest-margin asset at a price (DKK 17.0bn) above the whole group's pre-deal market value (DKK 13.1bn).
  • Return capital, with no large-scale acquisitions ("To avoid any doubt ... we are not planning to do any large-scale acquisitions", CFO, Q1 2026).
  • Right-size overhead (DKK 200m).
  • Drive product cadence (Evolve3 across about half the Enterprise range; SteelSeries share in premium headsets).
  • Keep a supply chain that can be moved quickly.
  • Unclear choices. How RemainCo wins against bundlers (Logitech, HP, Dell, Lenovo); what role video collaboration and FalCom play; how much R&D the smaller company will fund (consensus capital expenditure, including capitalised development, falls from about 10.7% to about 4.6% of revenue by 2029); and whether Gaming and Enterprise belong together (C-41 and C-79).
  • Resourcing. Ample on capital and cost. Modest on growth: management says the strategy will prioritise R&D and supply chain "without requiring major capital investment step-ups".
  • Credibility cost. Management promoted One GN for two years and then abandoned it within months. That lowers how much weight the market will give the new strategy until RemainCo delivers.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F3668th of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: operating guidance has been lowered, not raised, and the long-term targets are being reset downwards. The capital-return ambition, by contrast, has been raised twice. For the investment case this is a rebasing that creates a lower, cleaner bar. The CMD is likely to confirm lower operating ambition than before.

  • FY2026 guidance.
  • Organic growth: 2-8% (February), cut to 0-6% (May), cut to 0-3% (August), confirmed in November.
  • Adjusted EBITA margin: reintroduced at 8-9% (May), raised to 9-10% (August). The raise came only from tariff refunds; Bernstein calculates the underlying midpoint fell 30bp.
  • Gaming was guided to the lower half of 7-13% (August). FalCom was cut from DKK 200m to DKK 150m (November).
  • Long-term targets. The 2024 CMD targets for the group, 5-8% organic growth and 16-17% EBITA margin, were suspended in March 2026. DNB expects new RemainCo targets of about 3-6% organic growth and about 12% EBITA margin through 2030.
  • Capital targets. The Q1 framework of excess cash of DKK 3.5-4.5bn by end-2027 became "at least DKK 4bn" in November, helped by the tax payment falling by about DKK 1.0-1.7bn. The dividend policy was reinstated at a 20-40% payout. The CFO's informal benchmark of about 2 points of margin from savings, taking the underlying EBITA run-rate to 10-11% in 2027, stands.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F4461st of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the perimeter is being reduced on a very large scale: Hearing, about 43% of 2025 group revenue, plus earlier consumer exits and non-core stakes that are candidates for sale. This is not the classic case of exiting low-return units. GN has exited its highest-return, most defensive unit and kept the lower-return cyclical ones. The change raises cash and simplicity and lowers average business quality.

  • What has gone or is going.
  • Hearing: 17.1% adjusted EBITA margin and +9% organic growth in Q1 2026, against RemainCo's 0.3% and -4%.
  • The Elite and Talk consumer earbud lines were wound down by end-2024, removing about DKK 600m of low-margin revenue for about DKK 200m of exit costs.
  • The Danish retailer Dansk HøreCenter was sold to Demant in 2024.
  • DNB flags the 19% Nations Benefits stake and Jabra Enhance as further disposal candidates. The roughly 9-10% LXE Hearing stake was written down in Q2 2026.
  • What remains. Two reporting divisions, outsourced manufacturing (7 of 9 sites), about 50% of revenue in Europe, 30% in North America and 20% in the rest of the world.
  • What the reduction does.
  • Positive: the model is simpler to analyse; leverage and interest disappear; conglomerate overhead (One GN) can be removed.
  • Negative: the absolute cyclicality is higher (Jefferies: RemainCo is "highly cyclical"); there are dis-synergies (DKK 200m stranded costs; FalCom had drawn on Hearing's miniaturisation know-how); and the one cash stream that held up through the cycle is gone.
  • Further reduction is possible. DNB speculates that Enterprise or Gaming could eventually be sold. Hold this as an option, not a base case.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5354th of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: GN is entering few new markets. The ones it is pursuing are mostly sensible adjacencies but small. They are video collaboration (a close adjacency with real cross-sell), FalCom tactical communications (a sensible mid-range adjacency) and SteelSeries category extension. None will change the growth rate before 2028.

  • Video collaboration (meeting rooms).
  • The addressable market is DKK 18bn, where GN's share is only 3-4%, against Logitech's 25-30% (over 50% in video bars) and Cisco's 33%.
  • 92% of IT buyers decide on both headsets and room video, and over two-thirds of GN's top 300 customers are trialling Jabra video. On the Wisdom Library's core-versus-adjacency test, this shares customers and channels with the core, so it is a "close" adjacency.
  • The DKK 63m write-down in Q4 2025, after partnering with Huddly for large rooms, shows realism about scale.
  • FalCom. It targets a DKK 5bn global market for dismounted soldier communications and broke through commercially in 2025 with about DKK 200m of revenue. It shares acoustic and hearing-protection know-how with the core. Contracts are lumpy, and losing the Hearing miniaturisation link may slow product development.
  • SteelSeries. Expansion into keyboards, mice and GG software, and premium audio (Arctis Nova Pro Omni, GameBuds). Mice suffer from an ageing portfolio.
  • Geography. Growth comes from North America and APAC without new country entries.
  • Jabra Enhance (direct-to-consumer hearing) has delayed its breakeven to late 2026 or early 2027. It sits outside the core and is a disposal candidate.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C+5521st of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the price rises so far have been nominal. About 10% in the US in mid-2025 passed tariff costs on to customers rather than raising prices in real terms. A real-terms element is latent and emerging, because tariff costs are now falling (refunds, relocation) faster than prices are being given back. Management increasingly claims pricing power in Enterprise; Gaming pricing power is admittedly weak.

  • The arithmetic of pass-through, on my estimates. North America is about 30% of RemainCo revenue and cost of goods about 50% of revenue. A tariff of about 20% on US-bound cost of goods therefore adds about 3% of revenue in cost. A 10% price rise on about 30% of revenue adds about 3% of revenue. The two roughly cancel.
  • Evidence of more than pass-through.
  • Enterprise underlying gross margin rose 110bp to 57.2% in Q2 2026, on "pricing discipline and lower tariffs".
  • The volume impact of the price rises was "well below the price gains" (CEO; DNB).
  • CDW says recent retail discounting reflects distributors adjusting their own margins, not cuts in GN's prices.
  • RemainCo underlying gross margin was about 51.7-51.9% in Q2-Q3 2026, against 49.6% in FY2025.
  • Where it becomes real. The DKK 153m tariff refund in Q3 2026 means part of the cost GN priced for has been reversed. If list prices hold and the refunded tariffs are not reimposed in the same form, the 2025 nominal rise becomes a real price gain. That is the latent element.
  • Limits.
  • Gaming has high consumer price elasticity (management). Its Q2 gross-margin gain of 520bp to 39.2% included releases of provisions.
  • European entry-level headsets face Yealink and Chinese pricing.
  • Distributors demand marketing funds, which causes "modest ASP erosion".
  • Bear-case reports warn that price-led gross-margin gains can mask volume declines.

For the model: price contributes about +1-2% a year in Enterprise premium and near 0% in Gaming. Keep a downside case in which part of the 2025 increase is given back in Europe.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C5529th of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: GN is not stepping up M&A. It has publicly ruled out large acquisitions and is a seller, not a buyer. Given its record, that is a positive change.

  • Record. The DKK 8bn SteelSeries acquisition (agreed late 2021, closed 2022) was bought near a cyclical peak for gaming. It drove leverage to 5.7-7.1x, forced dividends to be suspended from FY2022 and an equity raise in 2023 (the share count rose from 128.0m to 145.6m), and its ERP integration delayed deliveries.
  • Since then. No material acquisition. The 2025 cash flow shows DKK 27m of acquisitions.
  • Now. The CFO said: "we are not planning to do any large-scale acquisitions". DNB names "wasting excess capital on value-destructive acquisitions" as a major risk for RemainCo, so the market will reward continued abstinence.
  • What would be sensible. Small technology tuck-ins (AI audio, video software) or partnerships of the Huddly kind.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

A861st of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: GN is turning from forced deleveraging to one of the largest relative capital-return programmes among European mid-caps. At least DKK 4bn by end-2027 is about 24% of the market value at DKK 114.9. The dividend has been reinstated, and the structure allows a second wave in 2028 from selling the Amplifon shares. This is the strongest positive change in the case. Its weakness is that it partly re-levers a cyclical business.

  • The programme.
  • A Safe Harbour buyback of up to DKK 1bn from closing to March 2027.
  • A further DKK 3bn for which authorisation will be sought at the 2027 AGM. Treasury shares are capped at 10%; holdings are about 3.5% today, so cancellations will be needed.
  • Dividends of 20-40% of net profit.
  • A gearing target of 1.0-1.5x net debt to EBITDA, which implies returning more than the net cash.
  • What enabled it. The upfront tax on the sale fell from DKK 1.5-2.0bn to DKK 300-500m.
  • Funding. At least DKK 4bn by end-2027 exceeds my estimate of net cash excluding Amplifon (about DKK 2.5-4bn). It is reachable only through some combination of re-levering towards 1.0-1.5x and selling Amplifon shares once lock-ups expire (9-15 months after closing). Management has said GN will not remain an anchor shareholder in Amplifon.
  • Per-share mechanics. At DKK 115-125 a share, DKK 4bn retires about 32-35m of 145.6m shares (22-24%) by end-2027. A second wave funded by Amplifon proceeds could follow in 2028. The Wisdom Library's "leverage-to-EPS transmission" warns that this lifts EPS growth mechanically and flatters the earnings CAGR relative to the quality of the business.
  • Risks.
  • Buying back at prices above intrinsic value. At about 9-10x 2027 EBITA for RemainCo, the buyback is no longer obviously below fair value.
  • Distributions depend on closing (near-certain) and on Amplifon's share price.
  • Re-levering into a downturn is GN's historical failure mode (2022).
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–60

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several further positive changes are present or plausible. Most are financial and mechanical rather than operational.

  1. Interest. Group net interest of DKK 645m in 2025 falls to near zero after closing, then rises modestly as GN re-levers.
  2. Tax. The smaller upfront tax payment keeps about DKK 1.0-1.7bn more liquidity. The equal-sized future tax asset shrinks, so the Consumer should model normal cash tax from 2027 rather than a multi-year shield.
  3. Amortisation. DKK 1.3bn of non-cash impairments in 2026, DKK 375m of them in RemainCo in Q1 against capitalised R&D and facilities, lower future amortisation. That mechanically supports adjusted EBITA from 2027, a classic "big bath" effect the Consumer should quantify.
  4. Non-core stakes. Nations Benefits (19%, DKK 0.5-2.0bn), LXE Hearing and Jabra Enhance could be monetised.
  5. Working capital. It is about 30-36% of RemainCo revenue (about 110 days, against about 45 at Logitech). Normalising it could release about DKK 1bn over time (C-52).
  6. Target reset. A CMD that sets realistic targets (about 3-6% growth, about 12% margin) could replace the over-promising pattern with a beatable bar.
  7. Strategic options. RemainCo or one of its divisions could become a target. DNB raises the possibility of a break-up. This is low-probability and speculative.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5023rd of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: no controlling actor has publicly signalled a change of strategy. The shareholder register shows real but passive influence, and the setup is prone to activism. The most consequential actor is William Demant Invest (about 12.3%), whose strategic reason to hold GN ended with the Hearing sale.

  • Who holds what. There is no activist in the AlphaSense library. Demant Invest holds over 10% and BlackRock over 5%. There is a single share class with equal votes and no controlling owner.
  • Evidence of shareholder influence.
  • In March 2023 large shareholders' lack of support forced management to withdraw a proposed rights issue of up to DKK 7bn. A smaller equity raise followed.
  • In 2022 the proxy advisers ISS and Glass Lewis recommended voting against the remuneration report.
  • The Q3 Earnings Review reads these episodes as having pushed the board towards returning capital.
  • Demant. Brokers long cited its stake as a consolidation catalyst for hearing. That catalyst is gone. At about DKK 2bn, the stake is smaller than the DKK 4bn buyback, so the buyback could absorb it. A sale would be an overhang if done in the market and neutral if done as a block into the buyback. This is conjecture; there is no stated intention.
  • Why the setup invites activism. The Wisdom Library pattern "value gap plus low insider ownership" fits: a sum-of-the-parts gap claimed by brokers, insiders below 0.15%, and a subscale business that DNB says could be broken up. If the CMD disappoints or the cash sits idle, outside pressure for faster returns or a sale becomes plausible.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C+6820th of 75

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: taken together, the positive corporate changes are strong and well resourced, but narrow in kind. They work mainly on the balance sheet and per-share mechanics (debt gone, cash returned, interest saved, share count down by over 20%, tax eased), plus one operational lever, the DKK 200m cost programme. They do not yet include an improvement in the franchise: growth, share or the structural margin gap to Logitech.

  • Strength. The force is large and dated: DKK 17.0bn crystallised, at least DKK 4bn returned by end-2027, and about 2 points of margin from savings in 2027.
  • Resourcing. Very high. The cash is contracted and regulators have cleared the deal.
  • Breadth. Medium. On the operating side only cost and supply-chain moves are firmly in hand; product cadence (Evolve3) is promising but unproven at volume.
  • Durability. The capital-return leg is mechanical once executed. The operating leg depends on demand.
  • Synthesis. The Q3 Earnings Review says it plainly: "The EPS case therefore rests on a smaller earnings base and a stronger balance sheet, not on a stronger business." The Consumer should keep the per-share bridge (share count, interest, tax) separate from the operating bridge (volume, price, mix, cost), because the first is high-confidence and the second is not.

A586th of 75

Judgement: GN has an unusually dense, dated staircase of positive developments over the next 18 months. Most of it is corporate and capital events with high foundation (closing, buybacks, dividend, CMD), followed by operating proofs with medium foundation (the savings showing up, Enterprise growth holding). The novelty of the near-term steps is moderate because the market already knows them. The genuinely novel steps are FY2027 guidance and the CMD targets in February 2027, and a second capital-return wave funded by Amplifon proceeds in late 2027 to 2028.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C-6029th of 75

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C-6029th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

GN leads a concentrated, certified, rationally priced core market. It holds about 46-50% of enterprise headsets against Poly's 29% and Logitech's 10%, competitors pass costs through cooperatively, and the premium margin pool is real (GN's gross margin is above Logitech's). That is the genuine structural support in the case.

Around that core, the value chain is less friendly:

  • GN is a subscale challenger in video and mid-pack in gaming.
  • Its distributors are concentrated (one at about a fifth of revenue) and are destocking under high rates.
  • Low-cost certified entrants and bundling PC makers are pressing from below and from the side.
  • Software noise suppression is a slow-acting substitute.
  • Precedents in the sector reward volume growth, not price, and punish the subscale specialist facing bundlers.

For the model:

  • Assume a stable premium headset share, a slow erosion at European entry level, and no price war in the core.
  • Treat distributor inventory as the main source of volatility from one quarter to the next.

The structure is supportive today and moderately fragile over three years.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F3870th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is a structural asset but a near-term hindrance. GN's understanding of IT distribution is the best in the industry, and its intermediaries now act as an amplifier of the cycle. The fragility is high because a single distributor accounts for about a fifth of RemainCo revenue.

  • Helps.
  • More than 20,000 commercial resellers.
  • Manufacturer-funded specialists embedded in partners such as CDW.
  • Corporate procurement requires certification from several vendors, which makes direct-to-corporate selling unviable for entrants and protects GN's channel position.
  • A former Jabra vice president says GN's reach through specialised distributors is "highly stable with no share loss".
  • Hinders.
  • European distributors keep "highly conservative inventory targets" under high rates.
  • Sell-in lagged sell-out in all regions through Q1 2026.
  • Large channel partners use their scale to demand marketing funds, causing modest average-price erosion.
  • Distributors discount to clear excess stock in mature regions.
  • Concentration. One distributor took DKK 2,001m in 2025, about 21% of RemainCo revenue on my arithmetic and higher as a share of Enterprise. A change in that distributor's stock policy can move a quarter's Enterprise revenue by several percent. The business-foundations report's early-warning signal is a sudden deterioration in distributors' working-capital cycles.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4447th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors' behaviour helps GN at the premium end (Poly's weakness) and hurts it in video, at European entry level and in gaming (Logitech's bundling and discounting, Yealink's pricing). On balance the effect slightly hurts. GN's premium headset position is robust; its video and lower-tier gaming positions are fragile.

  • Poly's integration under HP has slowed its innovation, giving Jabra share.
  • Logitech's strength in video (over 50% of video bars), its hardware-at-cost tactics and its premium execution in peripherals hurt GN's room-system ambitions and pressure SteelSeries.
  • Logitech's supply disruption helps SteelSeries temporarily.
  • Cisco dominates large-room video.
  • Relative position: robust in premium headsets (the specialist leader), fragile in video (5% of revenue against entrenched leaders) and mid-tier in gaming (share tied to product cycles).

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C7225th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: GN's core market is highly concentrated, with a steep share distribution. Gaming is moderately concentrated. Concentration also extends into the distribution layer, where it works against GN.

  • Enterprise headsets. The top three hold about 85% (Jabra 46%, Poly 29%, Logitech 10%) and the top four about 88%. The remaining 12% is fragmented among Chinese and unbranded makers. Both tests are passed easily: the top five hold far more than 30%, and far more than five times the share of numbers six to ten.
  • Video collaboration. The top three hold about 70% (Cisco, Logitech, Poly).
  • Core gaming gear. The top five hold about 69% (Logitech 22%, Razer 20%, HyperX 13%, Corsair 8%, SteelSeries 6%), with a long tail of Asian and boutique brands. That is less steep but still above five times the next five.
  • Distribution. IT distribution is concentrated (TD Synnex, Ingram Micro, Bechtle, Atea). One distributor bought DKK 2,001m from GN in 2025, about 21% of RemainCo revenue on my arithmetic. Distributors earn only 6-7% gross margins and are very sensitive to inventory, so they amplify GN's sell-in swings.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5546th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: enterprise headset shares have been broadly stable over three to five years, and where they moved, Jabra gained. Entrants (Chinese and unbranded makers, Yealink) hold about 12% and are gaining at the European entry level. Video and gaming shares move more, and favour Logitech.

  • Enterprise headsets. Experts (CDW, a former Poly engineering vice president, Logitech's head of B2B for Western Europe) agree Jabra took significant share from Poly after HP's messy integration and remains the clear global leader by revenue and units. In Q2 2026, Evolve3 drove "premium market share gains".
  • The entrant share is not quantified in the files over a three-to-five-year window. Two pieces of evidence point to recent erosion: GN's double-digit European decline in early 2026 is attributed to "increased competition in entry-level headsets", and Jabra's own experts say Yealink is closing the feature gap.
  • Video. Logitech's share is rising (to about 25%) and Poly's falling (to about 11%). GN is not gaining materially.
  • Gaming. SteelSeries is gaining in premium headsets (Nova Pro Omni) and losing in mice (an ageing portfolio).

For the model: a flat premium headset share; a European entry-level loss of 1-2 points a year as a reasonable assumption (my estimate, not sourced); gaming share tied to product cycles.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5243rd of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: so far, enterprise headset share has consolidated towards the most advantaged specialist, Jabra, not towards the largest bundler. The structural risk is that over a longer horizon share drifts towards players who can bundle across categories (Logitech, HP, Dell, Lenovo). In video and overall gaming, share is consolidating towards Logitech, and GN is not among the winners there.

  • Why the specialist has won. Certification and device management, channel embedding (manufacturer-funded specialists sitting inside resellers such as CDW), better design, and the bundler's own integration failure (Poly at HP).
  • Why the trend could reverse. The Wisdom Library entry on bundle moats records that, where two or three dominant players compete, the player able to bundle several products progressively wins 1-2 points of share a year against point-product specialists, "regardless of point-product quality". The hospital tenders it describes map onto multi-thousand-seat corporate IT procurement. DNB (November 2026) and the Q1 and Q3 Earnings Reviews all flag bundling as the structural threat over three years.
  • GN's counter. 92% of IT buyers decide on both headsets and room video, so Jabra can bundle within audio-visual. Its video share is too small to make that bundle compelling against Logitech.
  • Gaming. Logitech is widening its profitability lead (45.2% gross margin in its September 2026 quarter, rising market share in mice and keyboards).
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C+7020th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in the core enterprise headset market, competitors mostly put margin first and behave cooperatively on price. Industry prices did not fall when volumes fell in 2023-25; the 2025 tariff cost was passed through industry-wide. Aggression is concentrated in specific pockets (HP Poly after its acquisition, Yealink and Chinese entrants, Logitech's video tactics) and in lower-tier gaming.

  • Logitech. Margin and returns first overall (14-18% EBIT margin, net cash). It raised US prices by up to 25% in 2025. In video it discounts hardware to cost to win managed-services revenue, which is volume-first in that segment.
  • HP Poly. Volume-first after the acquisition (discounts of up to 80% to win corporate contracts). This damaged its brand and cost it share. It is now attempting about 5% average price increases.
  • Yealink and Chinese entrants. Volume-first, low-price, structural.
  • Distributors. They absorb discounting by adjusting their own margins (CDW).
  • Gaming. Razer holds list prices to protect the brand. Logitech and Corsair cut 10-15% in slowdowns. Asian copycats are 30-50% cheaper.
  • Does price fall when volume falls? Through 14 quarters of declining Enterprise volume, GN's Enterprise gross margin stayed about 55-57% and average prices showed only modest erosion. The answer is no in the core. In lower-tier gaming the answer is partly yes.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4445th of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors' attitude is only moderately stable, and it has become somewhat more aggressive over 18 months to three years. HP Poly has been erratic. Bundling and Chinese entry have grown. Logitech is rational but opportunistic. GN is not visibly weak at the premium end, which lowers the risk of a "shark attack". In Europe it is visibly weak, and that is where pricing pressure is greatest.

  • The Michael O'Leary test (hard to predict, reactive). HP Poly fits it: aggressive campaigns after the acquisition followed by integration disarray. Logitech does not; it follows a consistent margin-first model with targeted aggression in video.
  • Changes in attitude.
  • Logitech increased European promotional spending to block Chinese online expansion.
  • PC makers have intensified bundling.
  • Yealink moved into European B2B.
  • Tariff-era price increases show cooperation at industry level.
  • A shark attack on GN's pricing. It would most likely come from Logitech or Yealink in European mid-tier tenders. After closing, GN's net cash gives it the means to defend. Its fixed-cost structure means a price war would hurt GN more than Logitech (C-75).
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C-6032nd of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the industry structure supports GN in its core: a concentrated, certified, rationally priced market in which GN leads. That support is moderately fragile at its edges: entry-level commoditisation, bundling, software substitution, distributor power and Europe. Against the bundlers, the fragility is slow-acting. Against a demand freeze, it is fast-acting.

  • Supports.
  • Top-three share of about 85%.
  • Certification barriers. Compatibility with Teams, Zoom and Google takes years of joint engineering.
  • Rational pricing in the core.
  • Premium margin pools: the brand-and-technology stage earns 40-55% gross margins, against 6-7% for distributors and 10-15% for contract manufacturers.
  • GN's own gross margin (about 50-57%) above Logitech's (about 45-50%). The industry structure lets GN earn a premium price; GN's problem is its cost base (C-53).
  • Fragilities. The entry level (Yealink and others); bundling; software substitution (C-03, C-36); distributor concentration (C-33); and a slowdown in the value pool as replacement cycles lengthen.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F4060th of 75full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F4262nd of 75

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the threat from substitutes is moderate and growing slowly. The main substitutes are software noise suppression on laptops, consumer earbuds and shared room systems replacing personal headsets. The evidence is in expert commentary and CIO behaviour, not yet in GN's premium sales.

  • Software AI noise suppression in Teams, Zoom, macOS and Krisp, combined with better laptop microphones. A Deutsche Telekom senior vice president says enterprises increasingly deploy software suites such as Krisp.
  • Consumer earbuds (AirPods, Galaxy Buds). GN left consumer earbuds in 2024 and now meets them as a substitute for its own products.
  • Meeting-room systems in hot-desking offices. CIOs prioritise room infrastructure over personal headsets (DNB). GN plays here, but is subscale.
  • Mitigation. Physical acoustic isolation, enterprise encryption, device management and on-device neural-network chips cannot be replicated by software. Premium sell-out is growing.
